\displaystyle-2(\underline{q})^{-1}\partial_{q_{0}}S^{-1}_{L}(p,q)-(\underline{q})^{-2}\left[S_{L}^{-1}(p,\bar{q})-S^{-1}_{L}(p,q)\right].

The result for the right
F
-kernel follows using similar arguments.

∎

To show some Leibniz-like formulas for the Laplace operator, the Cauchy-Fueter operator and its conjugate we need some preliminary results.

Proposition 2.80
.

Let
f(x+iy)=\alpha(x,y)+i\beta(x,y)
be a holomorphic function in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
\Omega
is symmetric with respect to the
x
-axis and
\Omega\cap\mathbb{R}\neq\emptyset
. We assume that the functions
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy the even-odd conditions
\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(x,-y)
and
\beta(x,y)=-\beta(x,-y)
. Then in a suitable neighbourhood of
\Omega\cap\mathbb{R}
we have

(2.64)

\alpha(x,y)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}y^{2j}}{(2j)!}\partial_{x}^{2j}[\alpha(x,0)],

(2.65)

\beta(x,y)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}y^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}\partial_{x}^{2j+1}[\alpha(x,0)].

Moreover the series (
2.64
) and (
2.65
) converge uniformly on the compact subsets of
\Omega
.

Proof.

The function
f(x+iy)
is real-analytic in
y
around any point
x\in\Omega\cap\mathbb{R}
. Thus we have

(2.66)

f(x+iy)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{y^{j}}{j!}\partial_{y}^{j}[f(x)].

Using the fact that
f
is holomorphic we get

\partial_{y}[f(x+iy)]=i\partial_{x}[f(x+iy)].

Moreover since
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy the even-odd conditions we have that
f(x)=\alpha(x,0)
. Thus we can write the expression (
2.66
) as

\displaystyle f(x+iy)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(iy)^{j}}{j!}\partial_{x}^{j}\left[f(x)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(iy)^{j}}{j!}\partial_{x}^{j}\left[\alpha(x,0)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}y^{2j}}{(2j)!}\partial_{x}^{2j}\left[\alpha(x,0)\right]+i\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}y^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}\partial_{x}^{2j+1}\left[\alpha(x,0)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(x,y)+i\beta(x,y).

The uniform convergence of the above series is guaranteed by the fact that the function
f
is holomorphic.
∎

The above result can be easily extended to slice hyperholomorphic functions. Note that
x+iy
denotes a complex variable
z
, whereas in the result below, the quaternionic variable
q
is written as
u+Iv
.

Lemma 2.81
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line. Let us assume that
f(q)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
is a slice hyperholomorphic function in
U
. Then in a suitable neighbourhood of
U\cap\mathbb{R}
we can write the functions
\alpha(u,v)
and
\beta(u,v)
as

\alpha(u,v)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j}}{(2j)!}\partial_{u}^{2j}[\alpha(u,0)],

\beta(u,v)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}[\alpha(u,0)].

Proof.

The result follows by applying the slice operator, see (
2.50
), to the holomorphic function
f(x+iy)=\alpha(x,y)+i\beta(x,y)
, where
\alpha
and
\beta
are as in (
2.64
) and (
2.65
). See also
[
27
, Lemma 4.5]
.
∎

Lemma 2.82
.

Let
q=u+Iv
, with
u
,
v\in\mathbb{R}
and
I\in\mathbb{S}
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line. Let us assume that
f(q)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
is a slice hyperholomorphic function in
U
. Then we have

\lim_{v\to 0}Df(q)=-2\partial_{u}f(u).

Proof.

If
q\notin\mathbb{R}
, setting
v=|\underline{q}|
and
I_{\underline{q}}=\underline{q}/|\underline{q}|
, we can write the action of the Cauchy-Fueter operator to
f
as

Df(q)=\left(\partial_{u}\alpha(u,v)-\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)-\frac{2}{v}\beta(u,v)\right)+I_{\underline{q}}\left(\partial_{u}\beta(u,v)+\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)\right),

see e.g.
[
46
]
.
Using the fact that
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy the Cauchy-Riemann conditions we have

Df(q)=-\frac{2}{v}\beta(u,v).

To show the result we have to take the limit as
v
tends to zero of the above expression. By Lemma
2.81
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}Df(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\lim_{v\to 0}\frac{\beta(u,v)}{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\lim_{v\to 0}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j}}{(2j+1)!}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}[\alpha(u,0)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\partial_{u}\alpha(u,0).

Finally, by using Theorem
2.6
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}Df(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\partial_{u}\alpha(u,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{u}\left[f(u)+f(u)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\partial_{u}f(u).

∎

Theorem 2.83
.

Let
g
be a left slice hyperholomorphic function in an axially symmetric open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
. Then, for
q\in U
, we have

(2.67)

\Delta(qg(q))=q\Delta g(q)+2Dg(q),

(2.68)

\displaystyle D(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{q}Dg(q)-2g(q)

(2.69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle qDg(q)-2g(\bar{q}).

(2.70)

\displaystyle\bar{D}(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4g(q)+2q\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)-\bar{q}Dg(q)

(2.71)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g(q)+2g(\bar{q})+q\bar{D}g(q).

Proof.

To prove (
2.67
) we use Leibniz formula for the derivatives
\partial_{q_{0}}
and
\partial_{q_{i}}
, with
1\leq i\leq 3
, and we have

(2.72)

\partial_{q_{0}}^{2}(qg(q))=q\partial_{q_{0}}^{2}g(q)+2\partial_{q_{0}}g(q),

and

(2.73)

\partial_{q_{i}}^{2}(qg(q))=q\partial_{q_{i}}^{2}g(q)+2e_{i}\partial_{q_{i}}g(q).

Using (
2.72
) and (
2.73
) we deduce

\displaystyle\Delta(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\partial_{q_{0}}^{2}(g(q))+q\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{q_{i}}^{2}(g(q))+2\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)+2\sum_{i=1}^{3}e_{i}\partial_{q_{i}}g(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\Delta g(q)+2Dg(q).

We now show formula (
2.68
). By
[
77
]
we know that
\partial_{\underline{q}}(\underline{q}g(q))=-3g(q)-\underline{q}(\partial_{\underline{q}}g(q))-2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}g(q)
, where
\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}
is the Euler operator defined in (
2.41
). This implies that

(2.74)

\displaystyle D(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{q_{0}}(qg(q))+q_{0}\partial_{\underline{q}}g(q)+\partial_{\underline{q}}(\underline{q}g(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)+q_{0}\partial_{\underline{q}}g(q)-2g(q)-\underline{q}\partial_{\underline{q}}g(q)-2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}g(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)+\bar{q}Dg(q)-2g(q)-2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}g(q)-\bar{q}\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{q}Dg(q)-2g(q)+(2\underline{q}\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)-2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}g(q)).

Using the fact that
g
is a left slice hyperholomorphic function, and so it is in the kernel of the left global operator
V_{q,L}
in (
2.48
), see Remark also
2.64
, we have for
q\notin\mathbb{R}

(2.75)

\displaystyle 2\left(\underline{q}\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)-\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}g(q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\underline{q}\left(\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)-\underline{q}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}g(q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\underline{q}\left(\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)+\frac{\underline{q}}{|\underline{q}|^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}g(q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\underline{q}V_{q,L}(g)(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

By plugging (
2.75
) into (
2.74
) we get the result. Now we consider
q\in\mathbb{R}
. Since the function
qg(q)
is slice hyperholomorphic, by Lemma
2.82
we have that

\lim_{v\to 0}D(qg(q))=-2\partial_{q_{0}}(q_{0}g(q_{0}))=-2g(q_{0})-2q_{0}\partial_{q_{0}}g(q_{0}).

This proves formula (
2.68
) in the case
q\in\mathbb{R}
. In this case,
the equivalence between (
2.68
) and (
2.69
) is
immediate. It remains to prove the equivalence between (
2.68
) and
(
2.69
) for
q\notin\mathbb{R}
. This is equivalent to showing
that

(2.76)

\underline{q}Dg(q)=g(\bar{q})-g(q).

We write
g(q)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
, with
q=u+Iv
and we know from e.g.
[
77
, page 74]
that

Dg(q)=\left(\partial_{u}\alpha(u,v)-\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)-\frac{2}{|\underline{q}|}\beta(u,v)\right)+\frac{\underline{q}}{|\underline{q}|}(\partial_{u}\beta(u,v)+\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)),\qquad q\notin\mathbb{R}.

Since the function
g
is left slice hyperholomorphic, by using the Cauchy-Riemann conditions see (
2.4
), we get

(2.77)

Dg(q)=-\frac{2}{|\underline{q}|}\beta(u,v).

The even-odd conditions satisfied by
\alpha(u,v)
and
\beta(u,v)
give

(2.78)

g(\bar{q})-g(q)=-2\frac{\underline{q}}{|\underline{q}|}\beta(u,v).

Since (
2.77
) and (
2.78
) coincide we get the equality (
2.69
).

Now, we show (
2.70
). By using the fact that
D+\bar{D}=2\partial_{q_{0}}
and by (
2.68
) we get

\displaystyle\bar{D}(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\partial_{q_{0}}(qg(q))-D(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4g(q)+2q\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)-\bar{q}Dg(q).

To show (
2.71
) we use once again the fact that
D+\bar{D}=2\partial_{q_{0}}
and by formula (
2.69
), we have

\displaystyle\bar{D}(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\partial_{q_{0}}(qg(q))-D(qg(q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g(q)+2q\partial_{q_{0}}g(q)+2g(\bar{q})-qDg(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g(\bar{q})+2g(q)+q\bar{D}g(q).

∎

Remark 2.84
.

The Leibniz-like formulas in Theorem
2.83
can be generalized by replacing the monomial
q
with a quaternionic polynomial of any degree, with real coefficients, see
[
30
, Thm. 9.3]
for the Laplace operator, see
[
53
, Thm. 3.10]
for the Cauchy-Fueter operator and
[
38
, Thm. 3.9]
for the conjugate Cauchy-Fueter operator, respectively.

3.
Relation between the
*
-Taylor expansion and the spherical expansion

In this section we study a relation between the two different expansions in series of a slice hyperholomorphic function around a quaternion
p
. A first result is provided in
[
64
, Thm. 8.1]
, however the relation described in this paper is different.

We start by studying the regularity in the variable
p
of the building blocks of the spherical series, see (
2.20
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
p
,
q\in\mathbb{H}
. Then the function
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
, for fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is left slice polyanalytic of order
n+1
with respect to the variable
p
.

Proof.

The result follows from direct calculations and the polyanalytic decomposition, see Theorem
2.61
. Indeed

\begin{split}&Q_{p}^{n}(q)=(q^{2}-2p_{0}q+|p|^{2})^{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}{n\choose k}(q^{2}+|p|^{2})^{k}(-2p_{0}q)^{n-k}=\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{n\choose k}{{k}\choose\ell}q^{2(k-\ell)}|p|^{2\ell}(-2qp_{0})^{n-k}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{n\choose k}{{k}\choose\ell}(-1)^{n-k}q^{n+k-2\ell}(p+\bar{p})^{n-k}(p\bar{p})^{\ell}\\
\end{split}

from which, grouping
\bar{p}
on the left with maximum power
n
, the statement follows.
∎

In the sequel, we shall make use of the right global operator
V_{p,R}
, see in Remark
2.63
.
To ease the notation we denote it simply as
V_{p}
, and we write:

(3.1)

V_{p}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}+\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}p_{\ell}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\ell}}\right)\frac{\underline{p}}{|\underline{p}|^{2}},\qquad p\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an open set and let
f(p)
be a quaternionic-valued function of class
\mathcal{C}^{1}(U)
.
Then, for
p\in U\setminus(U\cap\mathbb{R})
, we have

(3.2)

V_{p}\left(f(p)p\right)=V_{p}(f(p))p.

In general, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in U\setminus(U\cap\mathbb{R})
we have that

(3.3)

V_{p}(f(p)p^{n})=V_{p}(f(p))p^{n}.

Proof.

From the Leibniz rule for the real derivatives we get

\displaystyle V_{p}\left(f(p)p\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\left(f(p)p\right)+\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}p_{\ell}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\ell}}(f(p)p)\right)\frac{\underline{p}}{|\underline{p}|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\left(f(p)\right)p+f(p)

\displaystyle+f(p)\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}p_{\ell}e_{\ell}\right)\frac{\underline{p}}{|\underline{p}|^{2}}+\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}p_{\ell}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\ell}}(f(p))\right)\frac{\underline{p}}{|\underline{p}|^{2}}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\left(f(p)\right)p+\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}p_{\ell}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\ell}}(f(p))\right)\frac{\underline{p}}{|\underline{p}|^{2}}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{p}(f(p))p.

Now, we prove formula (
3.3
) by induction on
n
. For
n=1
the result follows by (
3.2
). We suppose that the statement is true for
n
and we prove it for
n+1
. By using the inductive hypothesis and (
3.2
) we get

\displaystyle V_{p}\left(f(p)p^{n+1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{p}\left(f(p)p^{n}p\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{p}\left(f(p)p^{n}\right)p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{p}\left(f(p)\right)p^{n+1}.

∎

The repeated application of the right global operator
V_{p}

to the building blocks
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
, see (
2.20
), of the spherical series expansions
leads to a remarkable relation described below.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
m
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for
q\in\mathbb{H}
,
p\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
we have

(3.4)

V_{p}^{m}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)=(-2)^{m}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}Q_{p}^{n-m}(q)(q-p)^{m*_{q,L}},\qquad n\geq m.

Proof.

We show the result by induction on
m
. We start from the case
m=1
. By applying the derivative
\partial_{p_{0}}
to
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
, and then
\partial_{p_{\ell}}
, with
\ell=1,2,3
, we get

(3.5)

\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}Q_{p}^{n}(q)=2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)(p_{0}-q),

(3.6)

\ \frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\ell}}Q_{p}^{n}(q)=2np_{\ell}Q_{p}^{n-1}(q).

Hence (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) yield

(3.7)

\displaystyle V_{p}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)(p_{0}-q)+2n\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}p_{\ell}^{2}\right)Q_{p}^{n-1}(q)\frac{\underline{p}}{|\underline{p}|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)(p_{0}-q)+2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)\underline{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)(q-p).

Let us suppose that the statement holds for
m
and we prove it for
m+1
. For the sake of simplicity, below we indicate simply by
*
all the
*_{q,L}
-products. First we observe that
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
commutes with
q
and its powers. By the inductive hypothesis, the binomial theorem (see (
2.10
)), Lemma
3.2
and formula (
3.7
) we get

\displaystyle V_{p}^{m+1}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)

\displaystyle=V_{p}V_{p}^{m}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)

\displaystyle=2^{m}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n-m}(q)(p-q)^{*m}\right]

\displaystyle=2^{m}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n-m}(q)\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\ell}q^{\ell}p^{m-\ell}(-1)^{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=2^{m}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\ell}q^{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n-m}(q)p^{m-\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=2^{m}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\ell}q^{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n-m}(q)\right]p^{m-\ell}

\displaystyle=2^{m+1}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}(n-m)\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\ell}q^{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}Q_{p}^{n-m-1}(q)(p-q)p^{m-\ell}

\displaystyle=2^{m+1}\frac{n!}{(n-m-1)!}Q_{p}^{n-m-1}(q)\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\ell}q^{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}(p-q)p^{m-\ell}

\displaystyle=2^{m+1}\frac{n!}{(n-m-1)!}Q_{p}^{n-m-1}(q)\left(-q\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\ell}q^{\ell}p^{m-\ell}(-1)^{\ell}\right.

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\left.+\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\ell}q^{\ell}p^{m-\ell}(-1)^{\ell}p\right)

\displaystyle=2^{m+1}\frac{n!}{(n-m-1)!}Q_{p}^{n-m-1}(q)\left[-q(p-q)^{*m}+(p-q)^{*m}p\right]

\displaystyle=2^{m+1}\frac{n!}{(n-m-1)!}Q_{p}^{n-m-1}(q)\left[(p-q)^{*m}*(p-q)\right]

(3.8)

\displaystyle=2^{m+1}\frac{n!}{(n-m-1)!}Q_{p}^{n-m-1}(q)\left[(p-q)^{*(m+1)}\right],

which proves the statement.
∎

The application of the right global operator
V_{p}^{n}
to the second part of the spherical series gives the following result.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
m
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for
q\in\mathbb{H}
,
p\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
we have

(3.9)

V_{p}^{m}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right]=(-2)^{m}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}Q_{p}^{n-m}(q)(q-p)^{(m+1)*_{q,L}},\qquad n\geq m.

Proof.

We show the result by induction on
m
. If
m=1
, by Lemma
3.2
, the fact that
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
commutes with
q
, and formula (
3.7
) we get

\displaystyle V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(q)q\right]-V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(q)p\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle qV_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right]-V_{p}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right]p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)q(p-q)-2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)(p-q)p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)(-p^{2}+2qp-q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q)(q-p)^{*2}.

Supposing that the statement is true for
m
, following arguments similar to those used to prove (
3.8
), we can prove it for
m+1
.
∎

As a particular case of the previous results we have the following.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{H}
and
p\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

(3.10)

V_{p}^{n}(Q_{p}^{n}(q))=c_{n}(q-p)^{n*_{q,L}},

and

(3.11)

V_{p}^{n}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)=c_{n}(q-p)^{(n+1)*_{q,L}},

where
c_{n}:=2^{n}n!(-1)^{n}
.

Proof.

The result follows by taking
m=n
in (
3.4
) and (
3.9
). For
n=0
both formulas are trivially true, since
c_{0}=1
,
V^{0}_{p}
is the identity and
Q^{0}_{p}(q)=1
.
∎

Now we have all the tools to describe a relation between the truncated regular and spherical series. We prove the next theorem for
p\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, since for
p\in\mathbb{R}
the regular and the spherical series coincide.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be a given sequence. Define a sequence
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
by

(3.12)

\begin{cases}b_{0}=c_{0}a_{0}\\
b_{n}=c_{n}a_{2n}+c_{n-1}a_{2n-1},\ n\geq 1\end{cases}

where
c_{n}:=2^{n}n!(-1)^{n}
.
Then for every
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and every
q\in\mathbb{H}
we have

\sum_{n=0}^{N}(q-p)^{n*_{q,L}}b_{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}V_{p}^{n}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)a_{2n}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}V_{p}^{n}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)a_{2n+1},

the last sum being empty for
N=0
.

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity, below we indicate simply by
*
all the
*_{q,L}
-products. Using Corollary
3.5
and the relations (
3.12
), we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N}(q-p)^{*n}b_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{0}a_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}c_{n}(q-p)^{*n}a_{2n}

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=1}^{N}c_{n-1}(q-p)^{*n}a_{2n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N}c_{n}(q-p)^{*n}a_{2n}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}c_{n}(q-p)^{*(n+1)}a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N}V_{p}^{n}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)a_{2n}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}V_{p}^{n}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)a_{2n+1}.

This proves the assertion.
∎

4.
Representation formulas of the quaternionic fine structure of Dirac-type

In this section we show that functions which are axially harmonic or polyanalytic of order 2 or axially Fueter regular
satisfy a representation formula in the spirit of the one in Theorem
2.6
for slice hyperholomorphic functions.
This is indeed a peculiarity of these sets of functions, obtained by applying to slice hyperholomorphic functions the operators
D
,
\bar{D}
or
\Delta
, respectively.

4.1.
Harmonic case

The application of the Cauchy-Fueter operator to the monomial
q^{n}
leads to the formula

(4.1)

D(q^{n})=-2nH_{n-1}(q),\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

where

(4.2)

H_{n}(q)=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\bar{q}^{k-1}q^{n+1-k},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

see
[
16
,
30
]
.
The polynomials
H_{n}(q)
have remarkable properties, described below.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{H}
then the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
satisfy the following properties:

1)

H_{n}(q)
are axially harmonic.

2)

H_{n}(q)
are left slice polyanalytic of order
n+1
.

3)

H_{n}(q)
are an Appell-sequence with respect to the real derivative
\partial_{q_{0}}
, i.e.

(4.3)

\partial_{q_{0}}H_{n}(q)=nH_{n-1}(q),\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

4)

H_{n}(q)
satisfy the inequality

(4.4)

|H_{n}(q)|\leq|q|^{n}.

5)

If we assume that
q\notin\mathbb{R}
then

(4.5)

H_{n-1}(q)=-\frac{(\underline{q})^{-1}}{2n}\left[\bar{q}^{n}-q^{n}\right],\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

1)

The first statement is a direct consequence of the Fueter mapping theorem, indeed by formula (
4.1
) we have

\Delta H_{n}(q)=-\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\Delta Dq^{n+1}=0.

2)

By a change of index in the definition of the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
we have

H_{n}(q)=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\bar{q}^{\ell}q^{n-\ell}.

Since
\{q^{n-\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{n}
are slice hyperholomorphic, Theorem
2.61
gives the result.

3)

Since
\partial_{q_{0}}
is a real derivative we can apply the Leibniz rule, and so we get

\displaystyle\partial_{q_{0}}H_{n}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\partial_{q_{0}}(q^{n+1-k}\bar{q}^{k-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(n+1-k)q^{n-k}\bar{q}^{k-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}(k-1)q^{n+1-k}\bar{q}^{k-2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-k)q^{n-k}\bar{q}^{k-1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}kq^{n-k}\bar{q}^{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}q^{n-k}\bar{q}^{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle nH_{n-1}(q).

4)

By the definition of the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
, see (
4.2
), we have

|H_{n}(q)|\leq\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}|q|^{n+1-k}|q|^{k-1}=|q|^{n}.

5)

We know that

a^{n}-b^{n}=(a-b)\sum_{k=1}^{n}a^{n-k}b^{k-1},

if
a
and
b
commute. By choosing
a=q
and
b=\bar{q}
in the definition of the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
we have that

\displaystyle H_{n-1}(q)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bar{q}^{k-1}q^{n-k}=-\frac{(\underline{q})^{-1}}{2n}(\bar{q}^{n}-q^{n}).

∎

It turns out that axially harmonic functions in
\mathcal{AH}(U)
admit a representation formula.

Theorem 4.2

(Representation formula for axially harmonic functions)
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric domain. Let
f:U\to\mathbb{H}
be a slice hyperholomorphic function according to Definition
2.4
. We assume that
q=u+I_{\underline{q}}v\notin\mathbb{R}
. Then, for any
I\in\mathbb{S}
, the axially harmonic function
g=Df
can be written as

(4.6)

g(q)=Df(q)=-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(u-Iv)-f(u+Iv)\right],

and
g
is constant on each
[q]\subset\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
.
If
q=u\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(4.7)

g(u)=Df(u)=-2\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u).

Proof.

By hypothesis we can write the function
f
as

f(q)=\alpha(u,v)+I_{\underline{q}}\beta(u,v),\qquad q=u+I_{\underline{q}}v,

where
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy the conditions in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
).

If
q\notin\mathbb{R}
we can apply the Cauchy-Fueter operator as in
[
46
]
, or Corollary 3.3 in
[
78
]
,
so that we obtain

g(q)=Df(q)=\left(\partial_{u}\alpha(u,v)-\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)-\frac{2}{v}\beta(u,v)\right)+I_{\underline{q}}\left(\partial_{u}\beta(u,v)+\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)\right).

Using the fact that
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy the Cauchy-Riemann conditions and Theorem
2.6
, we get

\displaystyle Df(u+Iv)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{v}\beta(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-|\underline{q}|^{-1}I\left[f(u-Iv)-f(u+Iv)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(u-Iv)-f(u+Iv)\right].

As is well-known, see
[
43
]
the expression
I\left[f(u-Iv)-f(u+Iv)\right]=2\beta(u,v)
, see Theorem
2.6
, and depends on
u,v
only, and
-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}=-1/|\underline{q}|=-1/v
so
g
is constant on
[q]
.
If
q=u\in\mathbb{R}
the formula follows by Lemma
2.82
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

If
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
is an axially symmetric domain and
f,f_{1}:U\to\mathbb{H}
are slice hyperholomorphic functions such that
g=Df=Df_{1}
the representation formula (
4.6
) is not affected. Indeed by
[
30
, Thm. 5.10]
we can write
f_{1}(q)=f(q)+a
, where
a\in\mathbb{H}
and it is readily seen that the right hand side of (
4.6
) is unchanged.

Remark 4.4
.

The paper
[
78
]
studies formulas relating the Cauchy-Riemann operator, the
spherical Dirac operator, the differential operator characterizing slice regularity, and the
spherical derivative of a slice function. Our formula (
4.6
) can be deduced from the formula
[
78
, Proposition 3.2]
, point (b) which makes use of the spherical derivative, but our interpretation here is different.

The representation formula for axially harmonic functions in
\mathcal{AH}(U)
can alternatively be deduced from the integral representation in Theorem
2.75
.
In the next result the strategy of proof is based on the computation of residues, as is done in
[
43
]
to prove the Cauchy formula.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{H}
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain, let
I\in\mathbb{S}
and set
dp_{I}=dp(-I)
.
Suppose that
f
is a slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\overline{U}
.
Then, for
q=u+I_{\underline{q}}v\notin\mathbb{R}
, with
I_{\underline{q}}=\frac{\underline{q}}{v}
, the left axially harmonic function
g=Df\in\mathcal{AH}_{L}(U)
can be written as

g(q)=Df(q)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-1}(q)dp_{I}f(p)=-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(u-Iv)-f(u+Iv)\right],

where

\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-1}(q)=(p^{2}-2q_{0}p+|q|^{2})^{-1}.

Proof.

We start by observing that the zeros of the polynomial
p^{2}-2q_{0}p+|q|^{2}
are either a real point or a 2-sphere. If the zeros are not real, on any slice
\mathbb{C}_{I}
we have two zeros
p_{1,2}=u\pm Iv
. In this case we can calculate the residues in the points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
on the plane
\mathbb{C}_{I}
. We suppose that
I\neq I_{\underline{q}}
and we start with
p_{1}
. We set

p=u+Iv+\varepsilon e^{I\theta},

and we have that

\displaystyle p^{2}-2q_{0}p+|q|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{2}-v^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}e^{2I\theta}+2u\varepsilon e^{I\theta}+2Iv\varepsilon e^{I\theta}+2uvI

\displaystyle-2u^{2}-2uvI-2u\varepsilon e^{I\theta}+u^{2}+v^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}e^{2I\theta}+2Iv\varepsilon e^{I\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon e^{I\theta}\left(\varepsilon e^{I\theta}+2Iv\right).

Since
dp_{I}=\varepsilon e^{I\theta}d\theta
we have

\displaystyle\pi I_{1}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(\varepsilon^{2}e^{2I\theta}+2Iv\varepsilon e^{I\theta}\right)^{-1}\varepsilon e^{I\theta}d\theta f(u+Iv)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varepsilon^{-1}e^{-I\theta}\left(\varepsilon e^{I\theta}+2Iv\right)^{-1}\varepsilon e^{I\theta}d\theta f(u+Iv)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(\varepsilon e^{I\theta}+2Iv\right)^{-1}d\theta f(u+Iv).

Now, for
\varepsilon\to 0
and
q\notin\mathbb{R}
we get

\displaystyle\pi I_{1}^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(2Iv)^{-1}d\theta f(u+Iv)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-I\pi\frac{1}{v}f(u+Iv).

Recalling that
I_{\underline{q}}:=\frac{\underline{q}}{v}
we have

\displaystyle I_{1}^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-I\frac{1}{v}f(u+Iv)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}If(u+Iv).

With similar computations we show that the residue in
p_{2}
is

I_{2}^{0}=(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}If(u-Iv).

Finally, by the residue theorem we have

\displaystyle g(q)=Df(q)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-1}(q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(I_{1}^{0}+I_{2}^{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(u-Iv)-f(u+Iv)\right].

∎

It is also possible to write an axially harmonic function in terms of the Gamma-operator in (
2.42
).

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric domain. Let
f:U\to\mathbb{H}
be a slice hyperholomorphic function. Then for
q\notin\mathbb{R}
, the axially harmonic function
g=Df\in\mathcal{AH}_{L}(U)
can be written as

g(q)=Df(q)=-(\underline{q})^{-1}\Gamma_{\underline{q}}f(q).

Proof.

Assume that function
f
is left slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
q
; then it is in the kernel of the left global operator
V_{q,L}
, see (
2.48
). Precisely we have that

V_{q,L}(f)(q)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}+\frac{\underline{q}}{|\underline{q}|^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}\right)f(q)=0.

This implies

(4.8)

\partial_{q_{0}}f(q)=-\frac{\underline{q}}{|\underline{q}|^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}f(q).

Hence by (
2.40
) we conclude that

\displaystyle Df(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{q_{0}}f(q)+\partial_{\underline{q}}f(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{q}}{|\underline{q}|^{2}}\Gamma_{\underline{q}}f(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}\Gamma_{\underline{q}}f(q).

∎

4.2.
Axially polyanalytic functions of order
2

Firstly, we consider the conjugate Fueter operator applied to the monomial
q^{n}
which leads to the expression

(4.9)

\bar{D}q^{n}=2n\mathcal{P}_{2,n-1}(q),\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

where

(4.10)

\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)=q^{n}+\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\bar{q}^{k-1}q^{n+1-k},

see
[
51
,
52
]
. We prove some properties of the functions
\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\in\mathbb{H}
. Then the functions
\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)

are such that

1)

\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
are homogeneous of degree
n
.

2)

\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
are left axially polyanalytic of order 2 and left slice polyanalytic of order
n+1
.

3)

\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
can be written in terms of the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
, see (
4.2
), and the Clifford-Appell polynomials
\mathcal{Q}_{n}(q)
, see (
2.45
), i.e.,

(4.11)

\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)=(n+2)\mathcal{Q}_{n}(q)-q_{0}n\mathcal{Q}_{n-1}(q),

and

(4.12)

\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)=q^{n}+H_{n}(q).

4)

For
q\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
we can write
\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
as

(4.13)

\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)=q^{n}-\frac{(\underline{q})^{-1}}{2(n+1)}(\bar{q}^{n+1}-q^{n+1}).

Proof.

We separately prove the various points.

1)

The first point follows directly from the definition. Indeed, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(tq)=t^{n}\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
.

2)

From the Fueter mapping theorem, see Theorem
2.68
, we get that the functions
\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
are left axially polyanalytic of order 2. Then, the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{2,n}(q)
are left slice polyanalytic of order
n+1
is a consequence of Theorem
2.61
.

3)

Formula (
4.11
) can be deduced by
[
52
]
while formula (
4.12
) follows from the definition of the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
.

4)

For
q\notin\mathbb{R}
, formula (
4.13
) follows by inserting formula (
4.5
) in (
4.12
).

∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Combining formulas (
4.11
) and (
4.12
) we can write the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
in terms of the Clifford-Appell polynomials

H_{n}(q)=(n+2)\mathcal{Q}_{n}(q)-q_{0}n\mathcal{Q}_{n-1}(q)-q^{n}.

Remark 4.9
.

In quaternionic analysis if a function is both slice hyperholomorphic and axially Fueter regular then it is a constant.
In the polyanalytic case this is not true.
For instance the polynomials
\mathcal{P}_{2,1}(q)
are (left) axially polyanalytic of order 2
and are also (left) slice polyanalytic of order 2, see the second point of Theorem
4.7
.

Theorem 4.10
.

(Representation formula for axially polyanalytic functions of order
2
).

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric domain and let
f:U\to\mathbb{H}
be a slice hyperholomorphic function, see Definition
2.4
. Then if
q=u+I_{\underline{q}}v\notin\mathbb{R}
, for any
I\in\mathbb{S}
, the function
h=\bar{D}f\in\mathcal{AP}_{2}^{L}(U)
can be written as

(4.14)

\begin{split}h(q)=\bar{D}f(q)=&\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})\right]+I_{\underline{q}}I\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})\right]\\
&+(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})\right],\end{split}

where
q_{\pm I}=u\pm Iv
. If
q=u\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(4.15)

h(u)=\bar{D}f(u)=4\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u).

Proof.

Let us consider
q\notin\mathbb{R}
. Since
D+\bar{D}=2\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}
and
f
is slice hyperholomorphic,
\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}f(q)=f^{\prime}(q)
. Therefore we have

h(q)=\bar{D}f(q)=2f^{\prime}(q)-Df(q).

Thus by using Theorem
2.6
and Theorem
4.2
, we get

\displaystyle h(q)=\bar{D}f(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u+Iv)+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u-Iv)\right]+I_{\underline{q}}I\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u-Iv)-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u+Iv)\right]

\displaystyle+(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(u-Iv)-f(u+Iv)\right],

and this gives the statement.
Now, we assume that
q=u\in\mathbb{R}
. By (
4.7
) we get

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}h(u+Iv)=\lim_{v\to 0}\bar{D}f(u+Iv)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\lim_{v\to 0}f^{\prime}(u+Iv)-\lim_{v\to 0}Df(u+Iv)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial}{\partial u}(\alpha(u,0))+2\frac{\partial}{\partial u}(\alpha(u,0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u).

∎

Remark 4.11
.

Under the assumptions of the previous theorem, if
f_{1}:U\to\mathbb{H}
is another slice hyperholomorphic function such that
h=\bar{D}f_{1}
,
then the representation formula (
4.14
) remains unaffected. Indeed, by
[
52
, Thm. 3.14]
we know that
f_{1}(q)=f(q)+a
, where
a\in\mathbb{H}
.
It is evident that the right-hand side of (
4.14
) is unchanged by this modification.

Also in the polyanalytic case it is possible to obtain the representation formula by computing the integral that characterizes the axially polyanalytic functions of order
2
, see Theorem
2.77
. Here we state the result with a sketch of the proof.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{H}
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain, let
I\in\mathbb{S}
and set
dp_{I}=dp(-I)
.
Suppose that
f
is a slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\bar{U}
,
then for any
q=u+I_{\underline{q}}v\notin\mathbb{R}
, the function
h=\bar{D}f\in\mathcal{AP}_{2}^{L}(U)
can be written as

\displaystyle h(q)=\bar{D}f(q)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}P_{2}^{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})\right]

\displaystyle+I_{\underline{q}}I\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})\right]

\displaystyle+(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})\right],

where
q_{\pm I}=u\pm Iv
and the kernel
P_{2}^{L}(p,q)
is defined in (
2.58
).

Proof.

By Proposition
2.78
we have

\displaystyle h(q)=\bar{D}f(q)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}P_{2}^{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}S^{-1}_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)\right]

\displaystyle-\left(-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-1}(q)dp_{I}f(p)\right).

Finally by
[
43
, Theorem 2.8.2]
and Proposition
4.5
we get the final result.
∎

It is also possible to write the representation formula of axially polyanalytic functions of order 2 in terms of the Euler and Gamma operators as follows.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric domain. Let
f:U\to\mathbb{H}
be a slice hyperholomorphic function. Then the axially polyanalytic function (of order
2
)
h=\bar{D}f
can be written as

h(q)=\bar{D}f(q)=(\underline{q})^{-1}\left[2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}f(q)+\Gamma_{\underline{q}}f(q)\right],\qquad q\not\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

Let us write
q=q_{0}+\underline{q}
.
Since
D+\bar{D}=2\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}
we get that

\bar{D}f(q)=2\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}f(q)-Df(q).

Since the function
f
is slice hyperholomorphic the equality (
4.8
) holds. So by Proposition
4.6
we conclude that

\displaystyle\bar{D}f(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}f(q)-Df(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\left[2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}f(q)+\Gamma_{\underline{q}}f(q)\right].

∎

4.3.
Axially regular case

In this section we deal with axially regular functions. We begin by proving that any axially Cauchy-Fueter regular function
\breve{f}=\Delta f
, for some slice hyperholomorphic function
f
, satisfies a representation formula in terms of
f
. The function
f
is called Fueter primitive of
\breve{f}
. The fact that
f
exists is due to the surjectivity of the second map
in the Fueter mapping theorem onto the set of axially regular functions, see
[
40
]
.

Theorem 4.14

(Representation formula for axially Fueter regular functions)
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric domain, and
f:U\to\mathbb{H}
be a slice hyperholomorphic function according to Definition
2.4
. We assume that
q=u+I_{\underline{q}}v\notin\mathbb{R}
. Then, for any
I\in\mathbb{S}
, the axially Fueter regular function
\breve{f}=\Delta f
can be written as

(4.16)

\breve{f}(q)=\Delta f(q)=|\underline{q}|^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}\left(2|\underline{q}|\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q)-I\left(f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})\right)\right),

where
q_{\pm I}=u\pm Iv
. If
q=u\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(4.17)

\Delta f(u)=-2\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}\left[f(u)\right].

The formulas do not depend on the choice of the Fueter primitive
f
.

Proof.

We start by considering
q\notin\mathbb{R}
. By
[
40
]
we know that
\breve{f}=\Delta f
and following
[
45
,
85
]
(or
[
67
]
) we can compute
\Delta f
, where
f(q)=f(u+I_{\underline{q}}v)=\alpha(u,v)+I_{\underline{q}}\beta(u,v)
is a slice hyperholomorphic function, so

\Delta f=2\frac{\partial\alpha(u,v)}{\partial v}\frac{1}{v}+2I_{\underline{q}}\left(\frac{\partial\beta(u,v)}{\partial v}\frac{1}{v}-\frac{\beta(u,v)}{v^{2}}\right).

Now, by using the Cauchy-Riemann conditions we get

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Delta f(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{v}\frac{\partial\beta(u,v)}{\partial u}+2I_{\underline{q}}\frac{1}{v}\frac{\partial\alpha(u,v)}{\partial u}-2I_{\underline{q}}\frac{\beta(u,v)}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{1}{v}I_{\underline{q}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\alpha(u,v)+I_{\underline{q}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\beta(u,v)-\frac{1}{v}\beta(u,v)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2|\underline{q}|^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q)-|\underline{q}|^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})\right].

We now show the independence of the formula (
4.16
) from the choice of the Fueter primitive
f
of
\Delta f
. Let
f_{1}
be another Fueter primitive of
\Delta f
. We know from
[
37
, Corollary 1]
or
[
50
, Thm. 4.11]
that
f_{1}
differs from
f
by a slice hyperholomorphic function in the kernel of the Laplacian, namely by an affine function. Thus
f_{1}(q)=f(q)+qa+b
,
a,b\in\mathbb{H}
and so

\begin{split}\Delta f_{1}(q)&=|\underline{q}|^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}\left(2|\underline{q}|\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f_{1}(q)-I\left[f_{1}(q_{-I})-f_{1}(q_{I})\right]\right)\\
&=|\underline{q}|^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}\left(2|\underline{q}|\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q)+2a|\underline{q}|-I\left[f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})+q_{-I}a-q_{I}a\right]\right)\\
&=|\underline{q}|^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}\left(2|\underline{q}|\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q)+2a|\underline{q}|-I\left(f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})-2I|\underline{q}|a\right)\right)\\
\end{split}

which coincides with the right hand side of (
4.16
).

Now, we consider
q\in\mathbb{R}
and we compute the limit for
v\to 0
of
\Delta f(q)
. By Lemma
2.81
we have

(4.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}\frac{\partial\beta(u,v)}{\partial u}\frac{1}{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j}}{(2j+1)!}\frac{\partial^{2j+2}}{\partial u^{2j+2}}\left[\alpha(u,0)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}\left[\alpha(u,0)\right].

By using once again Lemma
2.81
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{v}\frac{\partial\alpha(u,v)}{\partial u}-\frac{\beta(u,v)}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{v}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\alpha(u,0)-\frac{1}{v^{2}}\left(v\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\alpha(u,0)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j-1}}{(2j)!}\frac{\partial^{2j+1}}{\partial u^{2j+1}}\alpha(u,0)-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j-1}}{(2j+1)!}\frac{\partial^{2j+1}}{\partial u^{2j+1}}\alpha(u,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j-1}}{(2j)!}\frac{\partial^{2j+1}}{\partial u^{2j+1}}\alpha(u,0)-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j-1}}{(2j+1)!}\frac{\partial^{2j+1}}{\partial u^{2j+1}}\alpha(u,0).

This implies that

(4.20)

\lim_{v\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{v}\frac{\partial\alpha(u,v)}{\partial u}-\frac{\beta(u,v)}{v^{2}}\right)=0.

Finally by (
4.20
) and (
4.19
) we get

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}\Delta f(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\lim_{v\to 0}\frac{\partial\beta(u,v)}{\partial u}\frac{1}{v}+2\lim_{v\to 0}I_{\underline{q}}\left(\frac{1}{v}\frac{\partial\alpha(u,v)}{\partial u}-\frac{\beta(u,v)}{v^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}\left[\alpha(u,0)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}\left[f(u)\right]

and the statement follows.
∎

Other representation formulas for axially Fueter regular functions can be found via their integral representation, see Theorem
2.72
. Here we consider the meaningful case
q\notin\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{H}
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain, let
I\in\mathbb{S}
and set
dp_{I}=dp(-I)
.
Then, for
q\notin\mathbb{R}
, an axially Fueter regular function
\breve{f}=\Delta f
, where
f\in\mathcal{SH}(V)
,
V\supset\bar{U}
, can be written as

(4.21)

\begin{split}\breve{f}(q)=\Delta f&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)\\
&=-2(\underline{q})^{-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q)+(\underline{q})^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})\right],\end{split}

where
F_{L}
is the kernel in (
2.52
) and
q_{\pm I}=u\pm Iv
. The formula is independent of the choice of the Fueter primitive
f
.

Proof.

By formula (
2.56
) and Theorem
2.77
we deduce that

\displaystyle-4\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-1}(q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)pdp_{I}f(p)-q\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)(p-q_{0})dp_{I}f(p)-\underline{q}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}P_{2}^{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)-\underline{q}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p).

So we get

\displaystyle\underline{q}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)=-\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}P_{2}^{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)+4\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}(q)^{-1}dp_{I}f(p).

Hence by Proposition
4.5
and Proposition
4.12
we have

\displaystyle\underline{q}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\pi\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})\right]-2\pi I_{\underline{q}}I\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})\right]

\displaystyle+2\pi(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})\right].

Finally we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{L}(p,q)dp_{I}f(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})\right]

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{-I})-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(q_{I})\right]

\displaystyle+(\underline{q})^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I})\right].

The independence of the choice of
f
follows from Theorem
2.72
applied to
f
.

∎

We now write the representation formula of
\breve{f}
in terms of the Euler and Gamma operators.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric slice domain and let
\breve{f}\in\mathcal{AM}(U)
be such that
\breve{f}=\Delta f
with
{f}\in\mathcal{SH}(U)
. Then the axially regular function
\breve{f}
can be written as

\breve{f}(q)=\Delta f(q)=|\underline{q}|^{-2}\left[2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}f(q)-\Gamma_{\underline{q}}f(q)\right],\qquad q\notin\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.14
and Theorem
4.10
we have

\Delta f(q)=-(\underline{q})^{-1}\bar{D}f(q)+2(\underline{q})^{-2}I_{\underline{q}}I\left(f(q_{-I})-f(q_{I}))\right),

and by Theorem
4.2
we get

\Delta f(q)=-(\underline{q})^{-1}\bar{D}f(q)-2(\underline{q})^{-1}Df(q).

Hence by Proposition
4.6
and Proposition
4.13
we can write

\Delta f(q)=-(\underline{q})^{-2}\left[2\mathbb{E}_{\underline{q}}f(q)-\Gamma_{\underline{q}}f(q)\right].

Since
(\underline{q})^{-2}=-|\underline{q}|^{-2}
we get the final result.
∎

The following result will play an important role when applying the operators
D
,
\Delta
, and
\bar{D}
to spherical series expansions.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric domain and let
f:U\to\mathbb{H}
be a slice hyperholomorphic function. Then, for
q\in U\setminus\mathbb{R}
, we have

(4.22)

Df(q)=(\underline{q})^{-1}\left[f(\bar{q})-f(q)\right].

(4.23)

\bar{D}f(q)=2\partial_{q_{0}}f(q)+(\underline{q})^{-1}\left[f(q)-f(\bar{q})\right].

(4.24)

\Delta f(q)=-2(\underline{q})^{-1}\partial_{q_{0}}f(q)+(\underline{q})^{-2}\left[f(q)-f(\bar{q})\right].

If
q=u\in U\cap\mathbb{R}
, then we have (
4.7
), (
4.15
) and (
4.17
), respectively.

Proof.

The result follows directly from Theorems
4.2
,
4.10
and
4.14
by choosing
I=I_{\underline{q}}
and using
I_{\underline{q}}^{2}=-1
, so that
f(u-I_{\underline{q}}v)=f(\bar{q})
,
f(u+I_{\underline{q}}v)=f(q)
,
|\underline{q}|^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}=-(\underline{q})^{-1}
and
|\underline{q}|^{-2}=-(\underline{q})^{-2}
.
∎

We note that, so far, we have considered the case of
left
slice hyperholomorphic (resp. axially harmonic, polyanalytic or regular) functions, but the discussion can be adapted with suitable modifications to treat the
right
slice hyperholomorphic case.

5.
Taylor series: axially harmonic functions

In this section we tackle the problem of writing a Taylor series of an axially harmonic function
Df\in\mathcal{AH}_{L}(U)
at a point
p
, using the fact that the slice hyperholomorphic function
f
around
p
can be expanded either using its
*
-Taylor or its spherical series.
We deduce that
Df
admits two different series expansions around
p
that we call harmonic regular and harmonic spherical expansions. As it happens for slice hyperholomorphic functions the two expansions have different sets of convergence.

5.1.
Harmonic regular series

To discuss an expansion for an axially harmonic function at a point
p\in\mathbb{H}
, we begin by giving the following definition:

Definition 5.1
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be an axially symmetric domain. Suppose
f
is a slice hyperholomorphic function (as in Definition
2.4
), which admits a
*
-Taylor expansion at
p
that converges in a suitable subset of
U
. Then the function
g=Df
, which is axially harmonic, is said to have a harmonic regular series at an arbitrarily fixed point
p
.

In order to give a precise expression of a harmonic regular series we compute the action of
D
on the building block
(q-p)^{*_{p,R}}=(q-p)^{*_{q,L}}
(see (
2.10
)) of the
*
-Taylor expansion.

To ease the notation, throughout this subsection, we denote the
*_{q,L}
-products simply as
*
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{H}
,
n\geq 1
and
D
be the Fueter operator in the variable
q
. Then:

(5.1)

D(q-p)^{*n}=-2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left[(q-p)^{*(n-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}\right],

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
n
. Since

D(q-p)=-2,

the result is trivial for
n=1
. So we suppose that the statement is valid for
n
and we prove that it holds for
n+1
.
Using the inductive hypothesis and formula (
2.69
) we get

\displaystyle D[(q-p)^{*(n+1)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D[(q-p)^{*n}*(q-p)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D[q(q-p)^{*n}-(q-p)^{*n}p]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D[q(q-p)^{*n}]-D[(q-p)^{*n}]p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2(\bar{q}-p)^{*n}-2q\sum_{k=1}^{n}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{k=1}^{n}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2(\bar{q}-p)^{*n}-2\sum_{k=1}^{n}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}*(q-p)*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2(\bar{q}-p)^{*n}-2\sum_{k=1}^{n}(q-p)^{*(n+1-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(q-p)^{*(n+1-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}.

This proves the result.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

If we take
p=0
in (
5.1
)
(note that we have to compute first the
*
-powers in
p
and then to evaluate the expression so obtained) we reobtain the expression in (
4.1
).

By setting

(5.2)

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p):=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(q-p)^{*(n+1-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)},\quad n\geq 0,

formula (
5.1
) can be written as

(5.3)

D(q-p)^{*n}=-2n\widetilde{H}_{n-1}(q,p).

The main properties of the functions
\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
, which are polynomials in the variable
p
, are summarized in the following result.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
,
p\in\mathbb{H}
. The polynomials
\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
satisfy the following properties:

1)

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
are left axially harmonic in
q
and right slice hyperholomorphic in
p
.

2)

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
are left slice polyanalytic functions of order
n+1
in
q
.

3)

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
form an Appell sequence with respect to the derivative
\partial_{q_{0}}
, i.e.

(5.4)

\partial_{q_{0}}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)=n\widetilde{H}_{n-1}(q,p),\qquad n\geq 1.

4)

If
q\notin\mathbb{R}
, then
\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
can be written as

(5.5)

\widetilde{H}_{n-1}(q,p)=-\frac{\underline{q}^{-1}}{2n}\left[(\bar{q}-p)^{*n}-(q-p)^{*n}\right].

Proof.

1)

Formula (
5.3
) yields

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)=-\frac{1}{2(n+1)}D(q-p)^{*(n+1)}

and since the function
(q-p)^{*(n+1)}
is left slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
q
, by the Fueter mapping theorem we get

\Delta\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)=-\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\Delta D(q-p)^{*(n+1)}=0.

From the formula (
2.10
) we immediately deduce the right slice hyperholomorphicity in the variable
p
.

2)

A change of index in the sum (
5.2
) gives

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*\ell}.

By the definition of the
*
-product and the binomial theorem we have

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}\bar{q}^{i}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}(-p)^{\ell-i},

and, collecting in the sum the terms for
\ell=0
and
i=0
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}\bar{q}^{i}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}(-p)^{\ell-i}+(q-p)^{*n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}\bar{q}^{i}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}(-p)^{\ell-i}\right.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.(q-p)^{*n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}p^{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}\bar{q}^{i}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}(-p)^{\ell-i}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}p^{\ell}\right).

We then collect the terms for
i=1
,
\ell=1
and we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{\ell=2}^{n}\sum_{i=2}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}\bar{q}^{i}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}(-p)^{\ell-i}+\bar{q}(q-p)^{*(n-1)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}p^{\ell}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{n}\bar{q}\binom{\ell}{1}(-1)^{\ell-1}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}p^{\ell-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{\ell=2}^{n}\sum_{i=2}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}\bar{q}^{i}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}(-p)^{\ell-i}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}p^{\ell}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\bar{q}\binom{\ell}{1}(-1)^{\ell-1}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}p^{\ell-1}\right).

By iterating the process we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\sum_{\ell=2}^{n}\sum_{i=2}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}\bar{q}^{i}(q-p)^{*(n-\ell)}(-p)^{\ell-i}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}p^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bar{q}\binom{k}{1}(-1)^{k-1}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}p^{k-1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{k=2}^{n}\bar{q}^{2}\binom{k}{2}(-1)^{k-2}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}p^{k-2}...+\bar{q}^{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\bar{q}^{\ell}\sum_{k=\ell}^{n}\binom{k}{\ell}(-1)^{k-\ell}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}p^{k-\ell}.

So we deduce

\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\bar{q}^{\ell}h_{\ell}(q),\qquad h_{\ell}(q):=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=\ell}^{n}\binom{k}{\ell}(-1)^{k-\ell}(q-p)^{*(n-k)}p^{k-\ell},

and since the functions
h_{\ell}(q)
are slice hyperholomorphic in
q
, by Theorem
2.61
we conclude that the polynomials
\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
are left slice polyanalytic of order
n+1
.

3)

To prove the statement we need to compute the action of
\partial_{q_{0}}
on the
*
-product. We first show that for
\ell,s\geq 1
we have

(5.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{q_{0}}\left[(q-p)^{*\ell}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*s}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ell(q-p)^{*(\ell-1)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*s}

\displaystyle+s(q-p)^{*\ell}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(s-1)}.

Formula (
2.10
) and the Leibniz formula yield

\displaystyle\partial_{q_{0}}\left[(q-p)^{*\ell}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*s}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{q_{0}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}q^{\ell-i}(-p)^{i}\right)*\left(\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{s}{j}\bar{q}^{s-j}(-p)^{j}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{\ell}{i}\binom{s}{j}\partial_{q_{0}}\left(q^{\ell-i}\bar{q}^{s-j}\right)(-p)^{j+i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{\ell}{i}\binom{s}{j}(\ell-i)q^{\ell-i-1}\bar{q}^{s-j}(-p)^{j+i}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{\ell}{i}\binom{s}{j}(s-j)q^{\ell-i}\bar{q}^{s-j-1}(-p)^{j+i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ell\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{\ell-1}{i}\binom{s}{j}q^{\ell-i-1}\bar{q}^{s-j}(-p)^{j+i}

\displaystyle+s\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{\ell}{i}\binom{s-1}{j}q^{\ell-i}\bar{q}^{s-j-1}(-p)^{j+i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ell\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell-1}{i}q^{\ell-i-1}(-p)^{i}\right)*\left(\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{s}{j}\bar{q}^{s-j}(-p)^{j}\right)

\displaystyle+s\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}q^{\ell-i}(-p)^{i}\right)*\left(\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{s-1}{j}\bar{q}^{s-j-1}(-p)^{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ell(q-p)^{*(\ell-1)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*s}+s(q-p)^{*\ell}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(s-1)},

for
\ell,s\geq 1
.
Now, we prove the desired formula (
5.4
). By using (
5.6
) (with
\ell:=n+1-k
and
s:=k-1
) we get the statement, in fact:

\displaystyle\partial_{q_{0}}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(n+1-k)(q-p)^{*(n-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}(k-1)(q-p)^{*(n+1-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-k)(q-p)^{*(n-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}k(q-p)^{*(n-k)}*(\bar{q}-p)^{*(k-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\widetilde{H}_{n-1}(q,p).

4)

By using (
5.3
), the binomial theorem, and formulas (
4.1
) and (
4.5
) we have:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{n-1}(q,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2n}D(q-p)^{*n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2n}D\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}q^{k}(-p)^{n-k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}k\binom{n}{k}H_{k-1}(q)(-p)^{n-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\underline{q}^{-1}(\bar{q}^{k}-q^{k})(-p)^{n-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\underline{q}^{-1}}{2n}\left[(\bar{q}-p)^{*n}-(q-p)^{*n}\right].

∎

Remark 5.5
.

If we consider
p=0
in (
5.5
) we get back the closed expression for the polynomials
H_{n}(q)
, see formula (
4.5
).

In the next result we show that the application of the Cauchy-Fueter operator to the
*
-Taylor series does not affect its set of convergence. We recall that the
*
-Taylor series converges in
\Omega(p,r)
, with
p\in\mathbb{H}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, see (
2.15
).

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{H}
and let
f
be a slice hyperholomorphic function in
U
and assume that
f
admits the
*
-Taylor expansion at
p\in U

(5.7)

f(q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(q-p)^{*n}a_{n}

convergent (absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets) in
\Omega(p,R)\subset U
, where
\frac{1}{R}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}|^{\frac{1}{n}}
. Then

(5.8)

Df(q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n},\qquad q\in\Omega(p,R),\quad b_{n}:=-2(n+1)a_{n+1}.

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity, below we indicate the
*_{q,L}
-products by
*
.
First we apply the Cauchy-Fueter operator to (
5.7
) term by term, and using formula (
5.3
) we get

(5.9)

\displaystyle Df(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D(q-p)^{*n}a_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\widetilde{H}_{n-1}(q,p)a_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)(n+1)a_{n+1}.

We now focus on the convergence of the series. If
q\in\Omega(p,R)\cap\mathbb{R}
the convergence of the series is immediate, so we assume that
q\notin\mathbb{R}
. Since
(q-p)^{*(n+1)}
is a slice hyperholomorphic function in
q
, by Theorem
4.2
and formula (
5.3
) we have

\displaystyle-2\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)(n+1)a_{n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D(q-p)^{*(n+1)}a_{n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[(q-p)_{|q=q_{-I}}^{*(n+1)}a_{n+1}-(q-p)_{|q=q_{I}}^{*(n+1)}a_{n+1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}I_{\underline{q}}I\left[(q_{-I}-p)^{(n+1)}a_{n+1}-(q_{I}-p)^{(n+1)}a_{n+1}\right],

where
p\in\mathbb{C}_{I}
and
q_{I}=u+Iv
,
q_{-I}=u-Iv\in[q]
are on that same plane. So we deduce that

|2\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)(n+1)a_{n+1}|\leq|\underline{q}|^{-1}\left[\left|(q_{-I}-p)^{(n+1)}a_{n+1}\right|+\left|(q_{I}-p)^{(n+1)}a_{n+1}\right|\right].

Since both the series

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|(q_{I}-p)^{(n+1)}a_{n+1}\right|,\qquad\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|(q_{-I}-p)^{(n+1)}a_{n+1}\right|,

are convergent in
\Omega(p,R)
, also the series (
5.9
) is convergent.

∎

Remark 5.7
.

If we consider
p=0
in (
5.8
) we get

(5.10)

g(q)=Df(q)=-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)H_{n}(q)a_{n+1},

since
\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,0)=H_{n}(q)
. Formula (
5.10
) is the Taylor expansion of
Df
in a neighbourhood of the origin.

The harmonic regular series can be written also in terms of the harmonic polynomials
H_{n}(q)
, as proved in the next result:

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
f
be a slice hyperholomorphic function in a neighbourhood of
p
that admits at
p
the
*
-Taylor expansion (
5.7
) with coefficients
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
, convergent (absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets) in
\Omega(p,R)
, where
\frac{1}{R}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}|^{1/n}
. Then the regular harmonic series of
Df
, for
q\in\Omega(p,R)
, can be written as

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}n\binom{n-1}{k}H_{k}(q)p^{n-k-1}(-1)^{n-k}a_{n},

where
b_{n}:=-2(n+1)a_{n+1}
,
{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Moreover, if
q\notin\mathbb{R}
we have

(5.11)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}=\underline{q}^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[(\bar{q}-p)^{*n}-(q-p)^{*n}\right]a_{n}.

Proof.

The assumption on the convergence on the compact subsets of
\Omega(p,R)
allows us to apply
D
term by term, and to rearrange the resulting double series. Thus, using (
2.10
), we can write

(5.12)

f(q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}q^{k}p^{n-k}(-1)^{n-k}a_{n}.

Applying the Fueter operator
D
to (
5.12
), and then using (
4.1
) and Theorem
5.6
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Df(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}kH_{k-1}(q)p^{n-k}(-1)^{n-k}a_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k-1}H_{k-1}(q)p^{n-k}(-1)^{n-k}a_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{k}H_{k}(q)p^{n-k-1}(-1)^{n-k}a_{n}.

We now suppose that
q\notin\mathbb{R}
. By formula (
5.5
) and the binomial theorem (applied to the
*
-product of slice functions) we get

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tilde{H}_{n}(q,p)(n+1)a_{n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[(\bar{q}-p)^{*(n+1)}-(q-p)^{*(n+1)}\right]a_{n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[(\bar{q}-p)^{*n}-(q-p)^{*n}\right]a_{n},

as asserted.
∎

We provide an example of a specific function that can be written as a harmonic regular series.

Example 5.9
.

In a neighborhood of the point
p+1
, the pseudo-Cauchy kernel, see (
2.54
), can be written in terms of the harmonic polynomials
\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)
:

(5.13)

\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-1}(q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p+1)a_{n},\qquad q\in\Omega(p+1,1),

where
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:=\{(-1)^{n}(n+1)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
The convergence of the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p+1)a_{n}
follows with arguments similar to those used in the proof of Theorem
5.6
. We observe that by applying term by term the Fueter operator to (
2.16
) and using (
5.3
) we have

\displaystyle DS^{-1}_{L}(p,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n+1}D(q-p-1)^{*n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}n\widetilde{H}_{n-1}(q,p+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}(n+1)\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p+1).

Since
\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-1}(q)=-\frac{1}{2}DS^{-1}_{L}(p,q)
, see (
2.55
), formula (
5.13
) follows.

5.2.
Harmonic spherical series

In this section we prove that there exists another series expansion of an axially harmonic function in a neighborhood of
p
whose set of convergence is open in the Euclidean topology.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
D
be the Cauchy-Fueter operator and
let
U
be an axially symmetric domain. If
f
is a slice hyperholomorphic function in a neighbourhood of
p\in U
where it admits a convergent spherical series expansion, then we say that
Df
has a harmonic spherical series expansion in a neighbourhood of
p
.

Definition
5.10
is motivated by Theorem
5.13
. To prove this theorem, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 5.11
.

For
p\in\mathbb{H}
and
q\in\mathbb{H}\setminus[p]
we set

(5.14)

\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\overline{Q_{p}^{\,n-1-k}(q)}Q_{p}^{k}(q),\qquad&n\geq 1\\
0,&n=0\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{|n|-1}\overline{Q_{p}^{-k-1}(q)}Q_{p}^{k+n}(q),&n\leq-1\end{cases}

The function
\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)
is real-valued, since
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)}=\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)
. We now establish a relation between
\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)
and

Q_{p}^{n}(q)
, see (
2.20
).

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{H}
and
q\in\mathbb{H}\setminus[p]
, then we have

(5.15)

Q_{p}^{n}(\overline{q})-Q_{p}^{n}(q)=-4(q_{0}-p_{0})\underline{q}\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p),\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

The result is trivial for
n=0
.
By (
2.20
) we have, for
n=1
,

(5.16)

Q_{p}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}(q)=\bar{q}^{\,2}-q^{2}-2p_{0}(\bar{q}-q)=(\bar{q}-q)(\bar{q}+q)-2p_{0}(\bar{q}-q)=-4(q_{0}-p_{0})\underline{q}.

Then, we prove the formula for
n>1
.
We use the well-known identity

(5.17)

A^{n}-B^{n}=(A-B)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}A^{n-1-k}B^{k},

valid whenever
A
and
B
commute.
Applying (
5.17
) with
A:=Q_{p}(\bar{q})
and
B:=Q_{p}(q)
, we obtain

(5.18)

Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}^{n}(q)=-4(q_{0}-p_{0})\underline{q}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\overline{Q_{p}^{n-1-k}(q)}Q_{p}^{k}(q).

Hence, by the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)
, (
5.15
) follows for

n\geq 1
.

We now consider the case
n\leq-1
. Set
m:=-n\geq 1
. If
A
and
B

commute, then

(5.19)

A^{-m}-B^{-m}=-(A-B)\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}A^{-k-1}B^{k-m}.

We apply (
5.19
) with
A:=Q_{p}(\bar{q})
and
B:=Q_{p}(q)
.
Therefore,

(5.20)

Q_{p}^{-m}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}^{-m}(q)=4(q_{0}-p_{0})\underline{q}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\overline{Q_{p}^{-k-1}(q)}Q_{p}^{k-m}(q).

By the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{-m}(q,p)
,
this proves (
5.15
) also for
n\leq-1
.

∎

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
f
be a slice hyperholomorphic function in an axially symmetric neighbourhood of
p\in\mathbb{H}
containing
U(p,R)
for some
R>0
where it admits a spherical expansion

(5.21)

f(q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)a_{2n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)a_{2n+1},\qquad\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subset\mathbb{H}

convergent (absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets) in
U(p,R)
.
Let
g
be the axially harmonic function in a neighbourhood of
p
given by
g=Df
.Then,
g
has the following harmonic spherical expansion

(5.22)

g(q)=-4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)(q_{0}-p_{0})[a_{2n}+(\bar{q}-p)a_{2n+1}]-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)a_{2n+1}

which can also be written as

(5.23)

g(q)=-4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)(q_{0}-p_{0})[a_{2n}+(q-p)a_{2n+1}]-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})a_{2n+1}

convergent (absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets) in
U(p,R)
.

Proof.

We apply the operator
D
term by term to (
5.21
). The interchange of
D
and the summation will be justified later in the proof. For
q\notin\mathbb{R}
, by (
4.22
) we have

D[Q_{p}^{n}(q)]=(\underline{q})^{-1}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right].

Using (
5.15
), this gives

(5.24)

D[Q_{p}^{n}(q)]=-4(q_{0}-p_{0})\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p).

Similarly,

D[Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)]=(\underline{q})^{-1}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})(\bar{q}-p)-Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right].

Since
q=\bar{q}+2\underline{q}
, we obtain

\displaystyle Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})(\bar{q}-p)-Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)

\displaystyle=\left[Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right](\bar{q}-p)-2\underline{q}\,Q_{p}^{n}(q).

Hence, by (
5.15
),

(5.25)

D[Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)]=-4(q_{0}-p_{0})\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)(\bar{q}-p)-2Q_{p}^{n}(q).

Therefore, applying (
5.24
) and (
5.25
) term by term to (
5.21
) yields (
5.22
). Alternatively, writing
q=\bar{q}+2\underline{q}
in the opposite way gives

(5.26)

D[Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)]=-4(q_{0}-p_{0})\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)(q-p)-2Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q}),

which yields (
5.23
) (recall also that
\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)
is real).
We now justify the interchange of
D
and the summation. Since, for
q\in K
compact subset of
U(p,R)
we have
\sup_{K}|Q_{p}(q)|=\rho^{2}<R^{2}
and
|\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|\overline{Q_{p}^{\,n-1-k}(q)}|\,|Q_{p}^{k}(q)|=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|Q_{p}^{\,n-1-k}(q)|\,|Q_{p}^{k}(q)|=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|Q_{p}(q)|^{n-1}=n\rho^{2n-2}
all the series in the previous calculations are convergent on the compact subsets of
U(p,R)
.

Finally, let
q=q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
and
Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)
are left slice hyperholomorphic in
q
(the first one being intrinsic), so that Lemma
2.82
gives
D(\cdot)=-2\partial_{q_{0}}(\cdot)
at real points; since

(5.27)

\partial_{q_{0}}Q_{p}^{n}(q_{0})=2n(q_{0}-p_{0})Q_{p}^{n-1}(q_{0})

we get

(5.28)

D\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q_{0})\right)=-4n(q_{0}-p_{0})Q_{p}^{n-1}(q_{0})

(5.29)

D\left[Q_{p}^{n}(q_{0})(q_{0}-p)\right]=-4n(q_{0}-p_{0})Q_{p}^{n-1}(q_{0})(q_{0}-p)-2Q_{p}^{n}(q_{0}).

On the other hand, for
q=q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
one has
Q_{p}(q_{0})\in\mathbb{R}
, whence
\mathcal{L}_{n}(q_{0},p)=nQ_{p}^{n-1}(q_{0})
and
\bar{q}=q
. Therefore, the expressions in
(
5.22
) and (
5.23
) reduce precisely to those obtained by applying
D
term by term at the real points. Hence, (
5.22
) and (
5.23
) hold also for
q=q_{0}\in U(p,R)\cap\mathbb{R}
.

∎

Our next goal is to study the convergence of the harmonic spherical series. To this end we recall the following result, see
[
84
, Lemma 2.3]
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
p_{0}
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
p_{1}>0
,
q\in\mathbb{H}
and
r\geq 0
be such that
|(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}|=r^{2}
. If
p=p_{0}+Ip_{1}
, for some
I\in\mathbb{S}
, then

\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}}-p_{1}\leq|q-p|\leq\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}}+p_{1}.

From this result we derive further estimates needed to study the convergence of a harmonic spherical series.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
p_{0}
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
p_{1}>0
,
q\in\mathbb{H}
and
r\geq 0
be such that
|(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}|=r^{2}
. If
p=p_{0}+Ip_{1}
, for some
I\in\mathbb{S}
, then

(5.30)

|q-p_{0}|\leq\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}},

(5.31)

|q_{0}-p_{0}|\leq\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}},

and

(5.32)

|\bar{q}-p|\leq\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}}+p_{1}.

Proof.

The inequalities (
5.30
) and (
5.31
) follow by the triangle inequality. Indeed we have

|q-p_{0}|^{2}=|(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}-p_{1}^{2}|\leq|(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}|+p_{1}^{2}=r^{2}+p_{1}^{2},

namely (
5.30
).
Since
|q_{0}-p_{0}|\leq|q-p_{0}|
we get the estimate (
5.31
). Finally, using
|\bar{q}-p_{0}|=|q-p_{0}|
,
|p-p_{0}|=|p_{1}|
, we have

\displaystyle|\bar{q}-p|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\bar{q}-p+p_{0}-p_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|q-p_{0}|+|p-p_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}}+p_{1},

which proves inequality (
5.32
).
∎

Recalling the definition of Cassini ball

{U}(p,R)
, see (
2.23
), we have the next result.

Theorem 5.16
.

With the notations in Theorem
5.13
,
let
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}|^{\frac{1}{n}}=\frac{1}{R},\qquad R>0.

The harmonic spherical series (
5.22
) converges (absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets) in

{U}(p,R)
.

Proof.

Let
K
be a compact subset of
{U}(p,R)
. By definition, if
q\in K
then it satisfies
|(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}|\leq r^{2}
for some
r
such that
0<r<R
. Fix
R^{\prime}
with
r<R^{\prime}<R
so that
|a_{n}|\leq(1/R^{\prime})^{n}
for
n
sufficiently large. This implies that for
n\geq 1
we have

|\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)|\leq n|Q_{p}(q)|^{n-1}\leq nr^{2n-2}.

Then by Theorem
5.13
, Lemma
5.14
and Lemma
5.15
we have

\displaystyle 4|\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)||q_{0}-p_{0}|[|a_{2n}|+|\bar{q}-p||a_{2n+1}|]+2|Q_{p}^{n}(q)||a_{2n+1}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4n}{r^{2}}\left(\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{r}{R^{\prime}}\right)^{2n}\left[1+\frac{1}{R^{\prime}}\left(\sqrt{r^{2}+p_{1}^{2}}+p_{1}\right)\right]+2\frac{1}{R^{\prime}}\left(\frac{r}{R^{\prime}}\right)^{2n}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle c_{n}.

Hence the harmonic spherical series in (
5.22
) is dominated by
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}
which is convergent and the statement follows.
∎

We can write the harmonic spherical series in terms of the harmonic polynomials
H_{n}
, see (
4.2
), as follows:

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
g
be the axially harmonic function in a neighborhood of
p=p_{0}+Ip_{1}\in\mathbb{H}
, with
I\in\mathbb{S}
,
p_{0}
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, given by
g=Df
where
f
is a slice hyperholomorphic function. Assume that
f
admits the spherical series expansion (
5.21
) around
p
with coefficients
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
, convergent (absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets) in a Cassini ball
U(p,R)
,
R>0
, as in Theorem
5.13
. Then, the harmonic spherical series expansion of
g
, for
q\in U(p,R)
, can be written as

(5.33)

\displaystyle g(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}2n\binom{n-1}{k}H_{2k+1}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k-1)}a_{2n}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(2k+1)H_{2k}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n+1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}n\binom{n-1}{k}H_{2k+1}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k-1)+1}Ia_{2n+1}\right).

Proof.

The hypothesis that
f
can be expanded as in (
2.21
), absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets of
U(p,R)
allows us, below, to apply
D
term by term, to expand each
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
by the binomial theorem and to rearrange the resulting series. We then apply the Cauchy-Fueter operator to the leftmost summation in (
2.21
) using the binomial theorem and (
4.1
). We get

(5.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(q-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}D\left((q-p_{0})^{2k}\right)p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}2kH_{2k-1}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k+1}(k+1)H_{2k+1}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k-1)}a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}n\binom{n-1}{k}H_{2k+1}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k-1)}a_{2n}.

We now apply the Cauchy-Fueter operator to the second summation of the expansion in series of
f
. By the binomial theorem and (
4.1
) we get

(5.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}D(q-p_{0})^{2k+1}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}D(q-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)+1}Ia_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(2k+1)H_{2k}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(2k)H_{2k-1}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k)+1}Ia_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(2k+1)H_{2k}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle+4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}n\binom{n-1}{k}H_{2k+1}(q-p_{0})p_{1}^{2(n-k-1)+1}Ia_{2n+1}.

Finally, the result follows by adding (
5.34
) and (
5.35
).

∎

Remark 5.18
.

In the hypothesis of Theorem
5.17
, by taking
p=0
in (
5.33
) we obtain

(5.36)

\displaystyle g(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(2n)H_{2n-1}(q)a_{2n}-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)H_{2n}(q)a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+2)H_{2n+1}(q)a_{2n+2}-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)H_{2n}(q)a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)H_{n}(q)a_{n+1}.

Formula (
5.36
) is the Taylor expansion in a neighbourhood of the origin of a function in
\mathcal{AH}_{L}(U)
, where
U
is a slice Cauchy domain containing the origin. The same result was obtained by taking
p=0
in the harmonic regular series, see Remark
5.7
.

The harmonic spherical series can also be written in terms of
Q_{p}^{n}(q)
and
Q_{p}^{n}(\overline{q})
in the next result, whose proof is given by direct calculations although it could be deduced also using the corresponding Representation Formula.

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
f
be a slice hyperholomorphic function in a neighbourhood of
p\in\mathbb{H}
having spherical expansion

(5.37)

f(q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)a_{2n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)a_{2n+1},\qquad a_{n}\in\mathbb{H}

convergent (absolutely and uniformly on the compact subsets) in
U(p,R)
, for some
R>0
.
Let
g
be the axially harmonic function in a neighborhood of
p
given by
g=Df
. Then, for
q\notin\mathbb{R}
,
g
has the following harmonic spherical expansion

(5.38)

g(q)=(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)a_{2n}\\
+(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})(\bar{q}-p)-Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)a_{2n+1},

which can also be written as

(5.39)

g(q)=(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})\left(a_{2n}+(\bar{q}-p)a_{2n+1}\right)\\
-(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)\left(a_{2n}+(q-p)a_{2n+1}\right).

Proof.

We apply term by term the Cauchy-Fueter operator
D
in the variable
q
to formula (
5.37
), by making use of the fact that, since
Q_{p}(q)=(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}
, we can write
Q_{p}^{n}(q)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(q-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}
.

More specifically, let us begin by applying the Cauchy-Fueter operator
D
to the leftmost summation in (
5.21
). From formulas (
4.1
) and (
4.5
) we have that

\displaystyle D\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)a_{2n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(q-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{n}{k+1}\binom{n-1}{k}\left[(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2(k+1)}-(q-p_{0})^{2(k+1)}\right]p_{1}^{2(n-k-1)}a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k+1}\left[(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2(k+1)}-(q-p_{0})^{2(k+1)}\right]p_{1}^{2(n-k-1)}a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left[(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2k}-(q-p_{0})^{2k}\right]p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left[(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2k}-(q-p_{0})^{2k}\right]p_{1}^{2(n-k)}a_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right]a_{2n}.

We then apply the Cauchy-Fueter operator
D
to the rightmost summation in (
5.21
). From formulas (
2.67
), (
4.1
) and (
4.5
) we get

\displaystyle D\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)a_{2n+1}\right)=D\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left(q(q-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}-(q-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}p\right)a_{2n+1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2k+1}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(q-p_{0})^{2k+1}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}\right)a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(q-p_{0})^{2k}p_{1}^{2(n-k)}\right)p_{1}Ia_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\left[(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}\right]^{n}(\bar{q}-p_{0})-\left[(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}\right]^{n}(q-p_{0})\right)a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle-(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left([(\bar{q}-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}]^{n}-[(q-p_{0})^{2}+p_{1}^{2}]^{n}\right)p_{1}Ia_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})(\bar{q}-p)-Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)a_{2n+1}.

Hence we have

\displaystyle Df(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)a_{2n}\right)+D\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)a_{2n+1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})-Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)a_{2n}

\displaystyle+(\underline{q})^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(\bar{q})(\bar{q}-p)-Q_{p}^{n}(q)(q-p)\right)a_{2n+1},

where, as in all the previous similar results, all the computations are justified by the uniform convergence on compact subsets of
U(p,R)
, and both series converge wherever (
5.37
) converges. The statement follows.
∎

A natural question to ask is whether there is a connection between the harmonic regular series and the harmonic spherical series. The answer is affirmative and involves the use of the right global operator introduced in (
3.1
).

Theorem 5.20
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{R}
and let
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be a given sequence. Define another sequence
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
by

(5.40)

b_{n-1}=-2n\left(c_{n}a_{2n}+c_{n-1}a_{2n-1}\right),\qquad n\geq 1,

where
c_{n}:=2^{n}n!(-1)^{n}
. Then the following relations between the truncated harmonic regular series and the harmonic spherical series hold

(5.41)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\sum_{n=0}^{N+1}V_{p}^{n}\left((q_{0}-p_{0})\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)\right)a_{2n}-4\sum_{n=0}^{N}V_{p}^{n}((q_{0}-p_{0})\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)(q-p))a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{N}V_{p}^{n}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(\overline{q})\right)a_{2n+1}.

and

(5.42)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\sum_{n=0}^{N+1}V_{p}^{n}\left((q_{0}-p_{0})\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)\right)a_{2n}-4\sum_{n=0}^{N}V_{p}^{n}((q_{0}-p_{0})\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,p)(\overline{q}-p))a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{N}V_{p}^{n}\left(Q_{p}^{n}(q)\right)a_{2n+1}.

where
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is fixed.

Proof.

By (
5.3
) we deduce that

\sum_{n=0}^{N}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}=-\sum_{n=0}^{N}D(q-p)^{*(n+1)}\frac{b_{n}}{2(n+1)}=-\sum_{n=1}^{N+1}D(q-p)^{*n}\frac{b_{n-1}}{2n}.

The relation (
5.40
) and Corollary
3.5
give

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N}\widetilde{H}_{n}(q,p)b_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N+1}D\left(c_{n}(q-p)^{*n}\right)a_{2n}+\sum_{n=1}^{N+1}D\left(c_{n-1}(q-p)^{*n}\right)a_{2n-1}
